| Chi parla | Battuta |  
|---|---|  
| *(didascalia)* | HARRY, MARY, IVY, VIOLET, GERALD, CHARLES |  
| VIOLET | Buonasera, Mary: non ti sei ancora cambiata?<br><br><br>Come ti sembra che stia Harry?<br>Ma chi può aver scostato quelle tende? |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Le riaccosta] |  
| VIOLET | Molto [[motifs/well\|bene]], mi pare, dopo un [[motifs/journey\|viaggio]] così lungo;<br>Sai che corsa ha dovuto fare per essere qui in [[motifs/time\|tempo]]<br>per il compleanno di sua madre. |  
| IVY | Mary, mia cara,<br><br><br>hai composto tu questi [[motifs/flowers\|fiori]]? Lascia che li cambi.<br>Non ti spiace, vero? Me ne intendo tanto di [[motifs/flowers\|fiori]];<br>i [[motifs/flowers\|fiori]] sono sempre stati la mia passione.<br>Sai che un tempo avevo un [[motifs/garden\|giardino]] mio, in Cornovaglia,<br>quando potevo permettermi un [[motifs/garden\|giardino]]; e vinsi parecchi premi<br>coi miei delphinium. Ero quasi un'autorità. |  
| GERALD | Buonasera, Mary. Hai già visto Harry, mi accorgo.<br><br><br>È bello riaverlo qui, non è vero?<br>Dobbiamo fargli sentire d'essere a [[motifs/home\|casa]]. E che buon auspicio<br>che abbia potuto esserci per il compleanno di sua madre. |  
| MARY | Devo andare a cambiarmi. Sono rientrata tardissimo. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Esce] |  
| CHARLES | Ora mancano soltanto [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] e John<br><br><br>Sono contento che sarete tutti insieme, Harry;<br>hanno bisogno dell'influenza del fratello maggiore.<br>[[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] è un poco sventato, lo sai;<br>tu dovresti fargli da freno.<br>In fondo, sei tu il capo della famiglia.<br>VOCE DI AMY. Violet! Sono arrivati [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] o John? |  
| VIOLET | Non è ancora arrivato nessuno dei due, Amy. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Entrano AMY e il DOTT. WARBURTON] |  
| AMY | È davvero seccante. Che cosa sarà successo?<br><br><br>Immagino sia la nebbia a trattenerli,<br>e non serve a nulla telefonare in giro. Harry!<br>Non hai visto il dottor Warburton?<br>Sai che è il più vecchio amico della famiglia,<br>e ti conosce da più tempo di chiunque altro, Harry.<br>Quando ha saputo che saresti stato qui a cena<br>ha rotto un impegno importante per venire. |  
| WARBURTON | Direi che siamo cambiati parecchio entrambi, Harry.<br><br><br>Un medico di campagna non ringiovanisce.<br>Rivederti mi riporta più indietro<br>di quanto tu possa ricordare. Ma non puoi aver scordato<br>il giorno che tornasti da scuola col morbillo<br>e quanta [[motifs/time\|fatica]] ci volle per tenerti a letto.<br>Non ti piaceva essere malato durante le vacanze. |  
| IVY | Era spiacevole tornare a [[motifs/home\|casa]] per prendersi una malattia. |  
| VIOLET | Fu sempre lo stesso coi tuoi malanni da poco<br><br><br>e le epidemie dei bambini: non restavi mai a letto<br>perché eri convinto che non saresti mai [[motifs/well\|guarito]]. |  
| HARRY | E non senza qualche ragione, io credo:<br><br><br>perché ciò che voi chiamate ritorno alla salute<br>non è che l'incubazione di un altro male. |  
| WARBURTON | Non devi prendere una veduta così pessimistica,<br><br><br>ché è poco lusinghiera per la mia professione.<br>Ma ricordo che, quando ero studente a Cambridge,<br>sognavo di fare qualche grande scoperta<br>per farla finita con questa o quella malattia.<br>Ora che ho quarant'anni d'esperienza<br>ho smesso di pensare in termini di laboratorio.<br>Siamo tutti malati in un modo o nell'altro:<br>chiamiamo salute il non trovare sintomo<br>di malattia. La salute è un termine relativo. |  
| IVY | Deve aver fatto un'esperienza ricchissima, dottore,<br><br><br>in quarant'anni. |  
| WARBURTON | Sì, davvero.<br><br><br>Anche in uno studio di campagna. Il mio primo paziente —<br>non ci credereste, signore — fu un assassino,<br>che soffriva d'un cancro incurabile.<br>Come lottò contro di esso! Non vidi mai un uomo<br>più ansioso di vivere. |  
| HARRY | Niente affatto straordinario.<br><br><br>È davvero più difficile credere all'assassinio<br>che credere al cancro. Il cancro è qui:<br>il nodulo, il [[motifs/pain\|dolore]] sordo, il male che torna a tratti:<br>l'assassinio, un rovesciarsi di [[motifs/sleep\|sonno]] e veglia.<br>L'assassinio era là. L'assassino comune<br>si considera una vittima [[archetypes/innocent\|innocente]].<br>Per sé è ancora ciò che era stato<br>o ciò che sarebbe. Non riesce a capire<br>che tutto è irrevocabile,<br>il passato irredimibile. Ma il cancro, quello sì,<br>quello è qualcosa di reale. |  
| WARBURTON | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], non parliamo di simili faccende.<br><br><br>Come siamo finiti sul tema del cancro?<br>Non lo so proprio. — Ma ora che siete tutti cresciuti<br>non mi resta un solo paziente a Wishwood.<br>Wishwood fu sempre un luogo freddo, ma sano.<br>Soltanto quando ricevo un invito a cena<br>mi capita di vedere vostra madre. |  
| VIOLET | Sì, guardate vostra madre!<br><br><br>Se non fosse che ora d'[[motifs/winter\|inverno]] non riesce a muoversi<br>non la direste d'un giorno più vecchia<br>che al suo compleanno di dieci anni fa. |  
| GERALD | Serve a qualcosa aspettare [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] e John? |  
| AMY | Tanto [[motifs/well\|vale]] andare a tavola.<br><br><br>Può darsi che arrivino prima che abbiamo finito. Mi accompagna, dottore?<br>Credo che siamo di gran lunga i più vecchi qui presenti —<br>anzi, siamo i più antichi abitanti.<br>Poiché venimmo per primi, per primi andremo a tavola. |  
| WARBURTON | Con piacere, Lady Monchensey,<br><br><br>e [[concepts/hope\|spero]] che l'anno venturo mi porti il medesimo [[concepts/honour\|onore]]. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Escono AMY, il DOTT. WARBURTON, HARRY] |  
| [[characters/chorus\|CORO]] | Ho paura di tutto ciò che è accaduto, e di tutto ciò che deve venire;<br><br><br>delle cose venture che siedono alla porta, come se fossero state là da sempre.<br><br><br>E il passato sta per accadere, e il futuro fu deciso da gran tempo.<br><br><br>E le ali del futuro oscurano il passato, il becco e gli artigli hanno profanato<br><br><br>la [[concepts/history\|Storia]]. Hanno svergognato<br>il primo grido nella camera, il chiasso nella stanza dei bambini, mutilato<br>l'album di famiglia, reso ridicolo<br>il pranzo dei fittavoli, il picnic di famiglia sulla brughiera. Hanno strappato<br>il tetto dalla casa, o forse non c'è mai stato.<br>E l'uccello siede sul comignolo spezzato. Ho paura. |  
| IVY | Questo è un terrore assai indegno, e devo lottare contro di esso. |  
| GERALD | Sono avvezzo al pericolo tangibile, ma solo a ciò che posso comprendere. |  
| VIOLET | È l'ottusità di Gerald e di Charles e di quel dottore, che mi dà ai nervi. |  
| CHARLES | Se la faccenda fosse lasciata nelle mie mani, credo che saprei governare la situazione. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Escono] |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Entra MARY, e passa oltre verso la cena. Entra [[characters/agatha\|AGATHA]]] |  
| [[characters/agatha\|AGATHA]] | L'occhio è su questa casa<br><br><br>l'occhio la ricopre<br>ce ne sono tre insieme<br>che i tre siano separati<br>che il nodo che fu stretto<br>venga sciolto<br>che le ossa incrociate<br>nel [[motifs/well\|pozzo]] colmato<br>siano infine raddrizzate<br>che la donnola e la lontra<br>attendano alle loro faccende<br>l'occhio del [[motifs/time\|tempo]] diurno<br>e l'occhio della [[motifs/night\|notte]], del suo [[motifs/time\|tempo]],<br>siano distolti da questa casa<br>finché il nodo non sia sciolto<br>l'incrociato disincrociato<br>e ciò che è storto raddrizzato. |  
| *(didascalia)* | [Esce verso la cena] |  
| [[characters/agatha\|AGATHA]] | FINE DELLA PARTE I |



<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_1" data-title="The Family Reunion — Part II, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/child,motif/autumn,motif/death,motif/home,motif/machine,motif/nature,motif/night,motif/pain,motif/road,motif/secrets,motif/silence,motif/sleep,motif/soul,motif/summer,motif/time,motif/well,motif/window,concept/deception,concept/doubt,concept/fear,concept/honour,concept/hope,concept/nature,concept/suffering,concept/wonder,setting/london,character/agatha,character/arthur,character/chorus"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | The library, after dinner. |
| *(didascalia)* | HARRY, WARBURTON |
| WARBURTON | I’m glad of a few minutes alone with you, Harry.<br><br><br>In fact, I had another reason for coming this evening<br>Than simply in [[concepts/honour\|honour]] of your mother’s birthday.<br>I wanted a private conversation with you<br>On a confidential matter. |
| HARRY | I can imagine —<br><br><br>Though I think it is probably going to be useless,<br>Or if anything, make matters rather more difficult.<br>But talk about it, if you like. |
| WARBURTON | You don’t understand me.<br><br><br>I’m sure you cannot know what is on my mind;<br>And as for making matters more difficult —<br>It is much more difficult not to be prepared<br>For something that is very likely to happen. |
| HARRY | O God, man, the things that are going to happen<br><br><br>Have already happened. |
| WARBURTON | That is in a sense true,<br><br><br>But without your knowing it, and what you know<br>Or do not know, at any moment<br>May make an endless difference to the future.<br>It’s about your mother … |
| HARRY | What about my mother?<br><br><br>Everything has always been referred back to mother.<br>When we were children, before we went to school,<br>The rule of conduct was simply pleasing mother;<br>Misconduct was simply being unkind to mother;<br>What was wrong was whatever made her suffer,<br>And whatever made her happy was what was virtuous —<br>Though never very happy, I remember. That was why<br>We all felt like failures, before we had begun.<br>When we came back, for the school holidays,<br>They were not holidays, but simply a [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>In which we were supposed to make up to mother<br>For all the weeks during which she had not seen us<br>Except at half-term, and seeing us then<br>Only seemed to make her more unhappy, and made us<br>Feel more guilty, and so we misbehaved<br>Next day at school, in order to be punished,<br>For punishment made us feel less guilty. Mother<br>Never punished us, but made us feel guilty.<br>I think that the things that are taken for granted<br>At [[motifs/home\|home]], make a deeper impression upon children<br>Than what they are told. |
| WARBURTON | Stop, Harry, you’re mistaken.<br><br><br>I mean, you don’t know what I want to tell you.<br>You may be quite right, but what we are concerned with<br>Now, is your mother’s happiness in the future,<br>For the [[motifs/time\|time]] she has to live: not with the past. |
| HARRY | Oh, is there any difference!<br><br><br>How can we be concerned with the past<br>And not with the future? or with the future<br>And not with the past? What I’m telling you<br>Is very important. Very important.<br>You must let me explain, and then you can talk.<br>I don’t know why, but just this evening<br>I feel an overwhelming need for explanation —<br>But perhaps I only dream that I am talking<br>And shall wake to find that I have been silent<br>Or talked to the stone deaf: and the others<br>Seem to hear something else than what I am saying.<br>But if you want to talk, at least you can tell me<br>Something useful. Do you remember my father? |
| WARBURTON | Why, yes, of course, Harry, but I really don’t see<br><br><br>What that has to do with the present occasion<br>Or with what I have to tell you. |
| HARRY | What you have to tell me<br>Is either something that I know already<br>Or unimportant, or else untrue.<br>But I want to know more about my father.<br>I hardly remember him, and I know very [[motifs/well\|well]]<br>That I was kept apart from him, till he went away.<br>We never heard him mentioned, but in some way or another<br>We felt that he was always here.<br>But when we would have grasped for him, there was only a vacuum<br>Surrounded by whispering aunts: Ivy and Violet —<br>[[characters/agatha\|Agatha]] never came then. Where was my father? |
| WARBURTON | Harry, there’s no good probing for misery.<br><br><br>There was enough once: but what festered<br>Then, has only left a cautery.<br>Leave it alone. You know that your mother<br>And your father were never very happy together:<br>They separated by mutual consent<br>And he went to live abroad. You were only a boy<br>When he died. You would not remember. |
| HARRY | But now I do remember. Not [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] or John,<br><br><br>They were too young. But now I remember<br>A [[motifs/summer\|summer]] day of unusual heat,<br>The day I lost my butterfly net;<br>I remember the [[motifs/silence\|silence]], and the hushed excitement<br>And the low conversation of triumphant aunts.<br>It is the conversations not overheard,<br>Not intended to be heard, with the sidewise looks,<br>That bring [[motifs/death\|death]] into the heart of a [[archetypes/child\|child]].<br>That was the day he died. Of course.<br><br><br>I mean, I suppose, the day on which the news arrived. |
| WARBURTON | You overinterpret.<br><br><br>I am sure that your mother always loved him;<br>There was never the slightest suspicion of scandal. |
| HARRY | Scandal? who said scandal? I did not.<br><br><br>Yes, I see now. That [[motifs/night\|night]], when she kissed me,<br>I felt the trap close. If you won’t tell me,<br>I must ask [[characters/agatha\|Agatha]]. I never dared before. |
| WARBURTON | I advise you strongly, not to ask your aunt —<br><br><br>I mean, there is nothing she could tell you. But, Harry,<br>We can’t sit here all the evening, you know;<br>You will have to have the birthday celebration,<br>And your brothers will be here. Won’t you let me tell you<br>What I had to say? |
| HARRY | Very [[motifs/well\|well]], tell me. |
| WARBURTON | It’s about your mother’s health that I wanted to talk to you.<br><br><br>I must tell you, Harry, that although your mother<br>Is still so alert, so vigorous of mind,<br>Although she seems as vital as ever —<br>It is only the force of her personality,<br>Her indomitable will, that keeps her alive.<br>I needn’t go into technicalities<br>At the present moment. The whole [[motifs/machine\|machine]] is weak<br>And running down. Her heart’s very feeble.<br>With care, and avoiding all excitement<br>She may live several years. A sudden shock<br>Might send her off at any moment.<br>If she had been another woman<br>She would not have lived until now.<br>Her determination has kept her going:<br>She has only lived for your return to Wishwood,<br>For you to take command at Wishwood,<br>And for that reason, it is most essential<br>That nothing should disturb or excite her. |
| HARRY | [[motifs/well\|Well]]! |
| WARBURTON | I’m very sorry for you, Harry.<br><br><br>I should have liked to spare you this,<br>Just now. But there were two reasons<br>Why you had to know. One is your mother,<br>To make her happy for the [[motifs/time\|time]] she has to live.<br>The other is yourself: the future of Wishwood<br>Depends on you. I don’t like to say this;<br>But you know that I am a very old friend,<br>And have always been a party to the family [[motifs/secrets\|secrets]] —<br>You know as [[motifs/well\|well]] as I do that [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] and John<br>Have been a great disappointment to your mother.<br>John’s very steady — but he’s not exactly brilliant;<br>And [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] has always been rather irresponsible.<br>Your mother’s hopes are all centred on you. |
| HARRY | Hopes? … Tell me<br><br><br>Did you know my father at about my present age? |
| WARBURTON | Why, yes, Harry, of course I did. |
| HARRY | What did he look like then? Did he look at all like me? |
| WARBURTON | Very much like you. Of course there are differences:<br><br><br>But, allowing for the changes in fashion<br>And your being clean-shaven, very much like you.<br>And now, Harry, let’s talk about yourself. |
| HARRY | I never saw a photograph. There is no portrait. |
| WARBURTON | What I want to know is, whether you’ve been sleeping … |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter DENMAN] |
| DENMAN | It’s Sergeant Winchell is here, my Lord,<br><br><br>And wants to see your Lordship very urgent,<br>And Dr. Warburton. He says it’s very urgent<br>Or he wouldn’t have troubled you. |
| HARRY | I’ll see him. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exit DENMAN] |
| WARBURTON | I [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] what he wants. I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] nothing has happened<br><br><br>To either of your brothers. |
| HARRY | Nothing can have happened<br><br><br>To either of my brothers. Nothing can happen —<br>If Sergeant Winchell is real. But Denman saw him.<br>But what if Denman saw him, and yet he was not real?<br>That would be worse than anything that has happened.<br>What if you saw him, and … |
| WARBURTON | Harry! Pull yourself together.<br><br><br>Something may have happened to one of your brothers. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter WINCHELL] |
| WINCHELL | Good evening, my Lord. Good evening, Doctor.<br><br><br>Many happy … Oh, I’m sorry, my Lord,<br>I was thinking it was your birthday, not her Ladyship’s. |
| HARRY | Her Ladyship’s! |
| *(didascalia)* | [He darts at WINCHELL and seizes him by the shoulders] |
| HARRY |                                               He is real, Doctor.<br><br><br>So let us resume the conversation. You, and I<br>And Winchell. Sit down, Winchell,<br>And have a glass of port. We were talking of my father. |
| WINCHELL | Always at your jokes, I see. You don’t look a year older<br><br><br>Than when I saw you last, my Lord. But a country sergeant<br>Doesn’t get younger. Thank you, no, my Lord;<br>I don’t find port agrees with the rheumatism. |
| WARBURTON | For God’s sake, Winchell, tell us your business.<br>His Lordship isn’t very [[motifs/well\|well]] this evening. |
| WINCHELL | I understand, Sir.<br><br><br>It’d be the same if it was my birthday —<br>I beg pardon, I’m forgetting.<br>If it was my mother’s. God rest her [[motifs/soul\|soul]],<br>She’s been dead these ten years. How is her Ladyship,<br>If I may ask, my Lord? |
| HARRY | Why do you keep asking<br><br><br>About her Ladyship? Do you know or don’t you?<br>I’m not afraid of you. |
| WINCHELL | I should [[concepts/hope\|hope]] not, my Lord.<br><br><br>I didn’t mean to put myself forward.<br>But you see, my Lord, I had good reason for asking … |
| HARRY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], do you want me to produce her for you? |
| WINCHELL | Oh no indeed, my Lord, I’d much rather not … |
| HARRY | You mean you think I can’t. But I might surprise you;<br><br><br>I think I might be able to give you a shock. |
| WINCHELL | There’s been shock enough for one evening, my Lord:<br><br><br>That’s what I’ve come about. |
| WARBURTON | For Heaven’s sake, Winchell,<br><br><br>Tell us your business. |
| WINCHELL | It’s about Mr. John. |
| HARRY | John! |
| WINCHELL | Yes, my Lord, I’m sorry.<br><br><br>I thought I’d better have a word with you quiet,<br>Rather than phone and perhaps disturb her Ladyship.<br>So I slipped along on my bike. Mostly walking,<br>What with the fog so thick, or I’d have been here sooner.<br>I’d telephoned to Dr. Warburton’s,<br>And they told me he was here, and that you’d arrived.<br>Mr. John’s had a bit of an accident<br>On the West [[motifs/road\|Road]], in the fog, coming along<br>At a pretty smart pace, I fancy, ran into a lorry<br>Drawn up round the bend. We’ll have the driver up for this:<br>Says he doesn’t know this part of the country<br>And stopped to take his bearings. We’ve got him at the Arms —<br>Mr. John, I mean. By a bit of luck<br>Dr. Owen was there, and looked him over;<br>Says there’s nothing wrong but some nasty cuts<br>And a bad concussion; says he’ll come round<br>In the morning, most likely, but he mustn’t be moved.<br>But Dr. Owen was anxious that you should have a look at him. |
| WARBURTON | Quite right, quite right. I’ll go and have a look at him.<br><br><br>We must explain to your mother …<br>AMY’S VOICE.                                        Harry! Harry!<br><br><br>Who’s there with you? Is it [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] or John? |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter AMY, followed severally by VIOLET, IVY, [[characters/agatha\|AGATHA]], GERALD and CHARLES.] |
| WARBURTON | Winchell! what are you here for? |
| WINCHELL | I’m sorry, my Lady, but I’ve just told the doctor,<br><br><br>It’s really nothing but a minor accident. |
| WARBURTON | It’s John has had the accident, Lady Monchensey;<br><br><br>And Winchell tells me Dr. Owen has seen him<br>And says it’s nothing but a slight concussion,<br>But he mustn’t be moved tonight. I’d trust Owen<br>On a matter like this. You can trust Owen.<br>We’ll bring him up tomorrow; and a few days’ rest,<br>I’ve no [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]], will be all that he needs. |
| AMY | Accident? What sort of an accident? |
| WINCHELL | Coming along in the fog, my Lady,<br><br><br>And he must have been in rather a hurry.<br>There was a lorry drawn up where it shouldn’t be,<br>Outside of the village, on the West [[motifs/road\|Road]]. |
| AMY | Where is he? |
| WINCHELL | At the Arms, my Lady;<br><br><br>Of course, he hasn’t come round yet.<br>Dr. Owen was there, by a bit of luck. |
| GERALD | I’ll go down and see him, Amy, and come back and report to you. |
| AMY | I must see for myself. Order the car at once. |
| WARBURTON | I forbid it, Lady Monchensey.<br><br><br>As your doctor, I forbid you to leave the house tonight.<br>There is nothing you could do, and out in this weather<br>At this [[motifs/time\|time]] of [[motifs/night\|night]], I would not answer for the consequences<br>I am going myself. I will come back and report to you. |
| AMY | I must see for myself. I do not believe you. |
| CHARLES | Much better leave it to Warburton, Amy.<br><br><br>Extremely fortunate for us that he’s here.<br>We must put ourselves under Warburton’s orders. |
| WARBURTON | I repeat, Lady Monchensey, that you must not go out.<br>If you do, I must decline to continue to treat you.<br>You are only delaying me. I shall return at once. |
| AMY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I suppose you are right. But can I trust you? |
| WARBURTON | You have trusted me a good many years, Lady Monchensey;<br><br><br>This is not the [[motifs/time\|time]] to begin to [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] me.<br>Come, Winchell. We can put your bicycle<br>On the back of my car. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exeunt WARBURTON and WINCHELL] |
| VIOLET | [[motifs/well\|Well]], Harry,<br><br><br>I think that you might have had something to say.<br>Aren’t you sorry for your brother? Aren’t you aware<br>Of what is going on? and what it means to your mother? |
| HARRY | Oh, of course I’m sorry. But from what Winchell says<br><br><br>I don’t think the matter can be very serious.<br>A minor trouble like a concussion<br>Cannot make very much difference to John.<br>A brief vacation from the kind of consciousness<br>That John enjoys, can’t make very much difference<br>To him or to anyone else. If he was ever really conscious,<br>I should be glad for him to have a breathing spell:<br>But John’s ordinary day isn’t much more than breathing. |
| IVY | Really, Harry! how can you be so callous?<br><br><br>I always thought you were so fond of John. |
| VIOLET | And if you don’t care what happens to John,<br><br><br>You might show some consideration to your mother. |
| AMY | I do not know very much:<br><br><br>And as I get older, I am coming to think<br>How little I have ever known.<br>But I think your remarks are much more inappropriate<br>Than Harry’s. |
| HARRY | It’s only when they see nothing<br><br><br>That people can always show the suitable emotions —<br>And so far as they feel at all, their emotions are suitable.<br>They don’t understand what it is to be awake,<br>To be living on several planes at once<br>Though one cannot speak with several voices at once.<br>I have all of the rightminded feeling about John<br>That you consider appropriate. Only, that’s not the language<br>That I choose to be talking. I will not talk yours. |
| AMY | You looked like your father<br><br><br>When you said that. |
| HARRY | I think, mother,<br><br><br>I shall make you lie down. You must be very tired. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exeunt HARRY and AMY] |
| VIOLET | I really do not understand Harry’s behaviour. |
| [[characters/agatha\|AGATHA]] | I think it is as [[motifs/well\|well]] to leave Harry to establish<br><br><br>If he can, some communication with his mother. |
| VIOLET | I do not seem to be very popular tonight. |
| CHARLES | [[motifs/well\|Well]], there’s no sort of use in any of us going —<br><br><br>On a [[motifs/night\|night]] like this — it’s a good three miles;<br>There’s nothing we could do that Warburton can’t.<br>If he’s worse than Winchell said, then he’ll let us know at once. |
| GERALD | I am really more afraid of the shock for Amy;<br><br><br>But I think that Warburton understands that. |
| IVY | You are quite right, Gerald, the one thing that matters<br><br><br>Is not to let her see that anyone is worried.<br>We must carry on as if nothing had happened,<br>And have the cake and presents. |
| GERALD | But I’m worried about [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]]:<br><br><br>He’s much more apt than John to get into trouble. |
| CHARLES | Oh, but [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]]’s a brilliant driver.<br><br><br>After all the experience he’s had at Brooklands,<br>He’s not likely to get into trouble. |
| GERALD | A brilliant driver, but more reckless. |
| IVY | Yet I remember, when they were boys,<br><br><br>[[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] was always the more adventurous<br>But John was the one that had the accidents,<br>Somehow, just because he was the slow one.<br><br><br>He was always the one to fall off the pony,<br>Or out of a tree — and always on his head. |
| VIOLET | But a year ago, [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] took me out in his car,<br><br><br>And I told him I would never go out with him again.<br>Not that I wanted to go with him at all —<br>Though of course he meant [[motifs/well\|well]] — but I think an open car<br>Is so undignified: you’re blown about so,<br>And you feel so conspicuous, lolling back<br>And so near the street, and everyone staring;<br>And the pace he went at was simply terrifying.<br>I said I would rather walk: and I did. |
| GERALD | Walk? where to? |
| VIOLET | He started out to take me to Cheltenham;<br><br><br>But I stopped him somewhere in Chiswick, I think.<br>Anyway, the district was unfamiliar<br>And I had the greatest trouble in getting [[motifs/home\|home]].<br>I am sure he meant [[motifs/well\|well]]. But I do think he is reckless. |
| GERALD | I [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] how much Amy knows about [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]]? |
| CHARLES | More than she cares to mention, I imagine. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter HARRY] |
| HARRY | Mother is asleep, I think: it’s strange how the old<br><br><br>Can drop off to [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] in the middle of calamity<br>Like children, or like hardened campaigners. She looked<br>Very much as she must have looked when she was a [[archetypes/child\|child]].<br>You’ve been holding a meeting — the usual family inquest<br>On the characters of all the junior members?<br>Or engaged in predicting the minor event,<br>Engaged in foreseeing the minor disaster?<br>You go on trying to think of each thing separately,<br>Making small things important, so that everything<br>May be unimportant, a slight deviation<br>From some imaginary course that life ought to take,<br>That you call normal. What you call the normal<br>Is merely the unreal and the unimportant.<br>I was like that in a way, so long as I could think<br>Even of my own life as an isolated ruin,<br>A casual bit of waste in an orderly universe.<br>But it begins to seem just part of some huge disaster,<br>Some monstrous mistake and aberration<br>Of all men, of the world, which I cannot put in order.<br>If you only knew the years that I have had to live<br>Since I came [[motifs/home\|home]], a few hours ago, to Wishwood. |
| VIOLET | I will make no observations on what you say, Harry;<br><br><br>My comments are not always welcome in this family. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter DENMAN] |
| DENMAN | Excuse me, Miss Ivy. There’s a trunk call for you. |
| IVY | A trunk call? for me? why, who can want me? |
| DENMAN | He wouldn’t give his name, Miss; but it’s Mr. [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]]. |
| IVY | [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]]! Oh dear, I’m afraid he’s had an accident. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exeunt IVY and DENMAN] |
| VIOLET | When it’s Ivy that he’s asking for, I expect the worst. |
| [[characters/agatha\|AGATHA]] | Whatever you have learned, Harry, you must remember<br><br><br>That there is always more: we cannot rest in being<br>The impatient spectators of malice or stupidity.<br>We must try to penetrate the other private worlds<br>Of make-believe and [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. To rest in our own [[concepts/suffering\|suffering]]<br>Is evasion of [[concepts/suffering\|suffering]]. We must learn to suffer more. |
| VIOLET | [[characters/agatha\|Agatha]]’s remarks are invariably pointed. |
| HARRY | Do you think that I believe what I said just now?<br><br><br>That was only what I should like to believe.<br>I was talking in abstractions: and you answered in abstractions.<br>I have a private puzzle. Were they simply outside,<br>I might escape somewhere, perhaps. Were they simply inside<br>I could cheat them perhaps with the aid of Dr. Warburton —<br>Or any other doctor, who would be another Warburton,<br>If you decided to set another doctor on me.<br>But this is too real for your words to alter.<br>Oh, there must be another way of talking<br><br><br>That would get us somewhere. You don’t understand me.<br>You can’t understand me. It’s not being alone<br>That is the horror — to be alone with the horror.<br>What matters is the filthiness. I can clean my skin,<br>Purify my life, void my mind,<br>But always the filthiness, that lies a little deeper … |
| *(didascalia)* | [Enter IVY] |
| IVY | Where is there an evening paper? |
| GERALD | Why, what’s the matter. |
| IVY | Somebody, look for [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] in the evening paper.<br><br><br>That was [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]], ringing up from [[settings/london\|London]]:<br>The connection was so bad, I could hardly hear him,<br>And his voice was very queer. It seems that [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] too<br>Has had an accident. I don’t think he’s hurt,<br>But he says that he hasn’t got the use of his car,<br>And he missed the last train, so he’s coming up tomorrow;<br>And he said there was something about it in the paper,<br>But it’s all a mistake. And not to tell his mother. |
| VIOLET | What’s the use of asking for an evening paper?<br><br><br>You know as [[motifs/well\|well]] as I do, at this distance from [[settings/london\|London]]<br>Nobody’s likely to have this evening’s paper. |
| CHARLES | Stop, I think I bought a lunch edition<br><br><br>Before I left St. Pancras. If I did, it’s in my overcoat.<br>I’ll see if it’s there. There might be something in that. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exit] |
| GERALD | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I said that [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] was every bit as likely<br><br><br>To have an accident as John. And it wasn’t John’s fault,<br>I don’t believe. John is unlucky,<br>But [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] is definitely reckless. |
| VIOLET | I think these racing cars ought to be prohibited. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Re-enter CHARLES, with a newspaper] |
| CHARLES | Yes, there is a paragraph … I’m glad to say<br><br><br>It’s not very conspicuous … |
| GERALD | There’ll have been more in the later editions.<br><br><br>You’d better read it to us.<br>CHARLES [reads].<br>‘Peer’s Brother in Motor Smash<br>The Hon. [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]] Gerald Charles Piper, younger brother of Lord Monchensey, who ran into and demolished a roundsman’s cart in Ebury Street early on the morning of January 1st, was fined £50 and costs to-day, and forbidden to drive a car for the next twelve months.<br><br><br>While trying to extricate his car from the collision, Mr. Piper reversed into a shop-[[motifs/window\|window]]. When challenged, Mr. Piper said: “I thought it was all open country about here” —’ |
| GERALD | Where? |
| CHARLES | In Ebury Street. ‘The police stated that at the [[motifs/time\|time]] of the accident Mr. Piper was being pursued by a patrol, and was travelling at the rate of 66 miles an hour. When asked why he did not stop when signalled by the police car, he said: “I thought you were having a game with me.”’ |
| GERALD | This is what the Communists make capital out of. |
| CHARLES | There’s a little more. ‘The Piper family …’ no, we needn’t read that. |
| VIOLET | This is just what I expected. But if [[characters/agatha\|Agatha]]<br><br><br>Is going to moralise about it, I shall scream. |
| GERALD | It’s going to be awkward, explaining this to Amy. |
| IVY | Poor [[characters/arthur\|Arthur]]! I’m sure that you’re being much too hard on him. |
| CHARLES | In my [[motifs/time\|time]], these affairs were kept out of the papers;<br><br><br>But nowadays, there’s no such thing as privacy. |
| [[characters/chorus\|CHORUS]] | In an old house there is always listening, and more is heard than is spoken.<br>And what is spoken remains in the room, waiting for the future to hear it.<br><br><br>And whatever happens began in the past, and presses hard on the future.<br><br><br>The agony in the curtained bedroom, whether of birth or of dying,<br>Gathers in to itself all the voices of the past, and projects them into the future.<br><br><br>The treble voices on the lawn<br>The mowing of hay in [[motifs/summer\|summer]]<br>The dogs and the old pony<br>The stumble and the wail of little [[motifs/pain\|pain]]<br>The chopping of wood in [[motifs/autumn\|autumn]]<br>And the singing in the kitchen<br>And the steps at [[motifs/night\|night]] in the corridor<br>The moment of sudden loathing<br>And the season of stifled sorrow<br>The whisper, the transparent [[concepts/deception\|deception]]<br>The keeping up of appearances<br>The making the best of a bad job<br>All twined and tangled together, all are recorded.<br>There is no avoiding these things<br>And we know nothing of exorcism<br>And whether in Argos or England<br>There are certain inflexible laws<br>Unalterable, in the [[concepts/nature\|nature]] of music.<br>There is nothing at all to be done about it,<br>There is nothing to do about anything,<br>And now it is nearly [[motifs/time\|time]] for the news<br>We must listen to the weather report<br>And the international catastrophes. |
| *(didascalia)* | [Exeunt [[characters/chorus\|CHORUS]]] |
